```php
// One row per Access row, as a select over the fixed capability list: the schema is the single
// place that knows what a row may be set to, and Access::defaults() the single place that knows
// what it starts as, so the two cannot drift.
it('declares one Settings › Access select per row, each defaulting to Access::defaults() and offering exactly Access::CAPABILITIES', function (): void {
```

`src/Access.php`:

```php
<?php

declare(strict_types=1);

namespace AlpacaBot;

use AlpacaBot\Settings\Store;

/**
 * Who may use what, per site: the rows of Settings › Access, each one capability name, and what a
 * row resolves to once code has had its say.
 *
 * A row's stored value is the *default its filter receives*, never a verdict of its own, so a site
 * that names a capability in code keeps it whatever the screen says. No role data is written and
 * no capability is invented: a row names one of CAPABILITIES, which core already hands out, and
 * user_can() answers it as it answers every other check. That is the whole of the access model —
 * there is no role × capability matrix and nothing is granted to a role.
 *
 * Rows and hooks. Every row but `chat` has a filter of its own, `alpaca_bot/capability/{hook}`,
 * where hook() turns the row's dots into slashes (`tool.web_fetch` is `tool/web_fetch`). `chat`
 * has none, and must not: it is the default of the hooks that were already there — the menu's
 * `alpaca_bot/admin/menu_capability` and each chat route's `alpaca_bot/capability/{route}` — and
 * those are filtered separately on purpose, so that a site can open the screen to a role and not
 * the API, or the reverse (Admin\Menu says why). effective('chat') is therefore the stored value,
 * and each surface applies its own filter over it.
 *
 * Two kinds of row are not in defaults(): `mcp.<server id>`, which exists once a server does, and
 * any row nobody declared (a typo, a toolkit a site registered in code). Both read as
 * `manage_options`, so a row this class has never heard of fails closed rather than open.
 *
 * Everything is read through Store, which reads `alpaca_bot_settings` at most once per request and
 * memoises it, so a request that asks every row costs no read beyond the one the settings already
 * cost (docs/reviews/2026-09-09-performance-baseline.md's one-read target).
 *
 * @since 0.6.0
 */
final class Access
{
    /**
     * The capabilities a row may name, most to least restrictive. A fixed list rather than
     * anything a site can extend: the point of a dropdown is that an operator cannot mistype a
     * capability into a surface that spends money, and a site that wants a capability of its own
     * names it in that row's filter, where Capability::filtered() still guards the answer.
     */
    public const CAPABILITIES = ['manage_options', 'edit_others_posts', 'publish_posts', 'edit_posts', 'read'];

    /** What a row defaults() does not list resolves to. */
    private const UNLISTED = 'manage_options';

    public function __construct(private Store $store) {}

    /**
     * Row => the capability it starts at. These reproduce 0.5 exactly: chat, the three built-in
     * tools and the shortcode are `edit_posts`, which is what all of those asked before there were
     * rows, so a site that never opens this tab sees no change. The abilities toolkit (0.6) and
     * both settings rows are `manage_options`. The tool rows are new gates rather than new
     * defaults: 0.5 had nothing between "may chat" and "may call the enabled tools", and the floor
     * that closes it is Toolkit\Registry::enabled()'s.
     *
     * @return array<string, string>
     */
    public static function defaults(): array
    {
        return [
            'chat' => 'edit_posts',
            'tool.web_fetch' => 'edit_posts',
            'tool.summarize' => 'edit_posts',
            'tool.draft_post' => 'edit_posts',
            'tool.abilities' => 'manage_options',
            'settings.read' => 'manage_options',
            'settings.write' => 'manage_options',
            'shortcode' => 'edit_posts',
        ];
    }

    /**
     * What the site has saved for `$row`, or the row's default. A stored value that is not one of
     * CAPABILITIES is the default too: Store hands back what is in the option, not what
     * Schema::sanitize() would make of it, so a hand-edited row, or one holding a capability that
     * has since left the list, must not become a check nobody chose.
     */
    public function stored(string $row): string
    {
        $value = $this->store->get('access.' . $row);
        return is_string($value) && in_array($value, self::CAPABILITIES, true)
            ? $value
            : (self::defaults()[$row] ?? self::UNLISTED);
    }

    /**
     * The capability `$row` resolves to for this call, code included: the stored row through its
     * filter. `$args` are passed on to the filter, so a surface hands it what it knows (the turn's
     * user id, the post and tag a shortcode is rendering in, the REST request being authorised).
     *
     * @param mixed ...$args extra arguments the row's filter receives after the capability
     */
    public function effective(string $row, mixed ...$args): string
    {
        $stored = $this->stored($row);
        if ($row === 'chat') {
            // No hook here on purpose: the class docblock says which two hooks carry this row.
            return $stored;
        }
        $hook = self::hook($row);
        /**
         * Filters the capability one row of Settings › Access resolves to. `{hook}` is the row with
         * its dots as slashes: `tool/web_fetch`, `tool/summarize`, `tool/draft_post` and
         * `tool/abilities` (the tool floor in Toolkit\Registry::enabled()), `mcp/{server id}`,
         * `settings/read` and `settings/write` (Rest\SettingsController) and `shortcode`
         * (Shortcodes\Chat). The value handed in is what the site saved for that row, so a filter
         * here beats the screen and the screen says so ("set in code"). Only a non-empty,
         * non-numeric string is honoured (Capability::filtered()): `__return_true` or a number
         * would become a legacy user-level check, so it is ignored and the stored row stands. The
         * Chat row has no filter here; the menu's `alpaca_bot/admin/menu_capability` and each chat
         * route's `alpaca_bot/capability/{route}` are what filter it, separately.
         *
         * @since 0.6.0
         * @param string $capability the row as the site saved it, its default when it never was
         * @param mixed  ...$args    what the asking surface passes: the turn's user id for a tool or MCP row, the post id and the shortcode tag for `shortcode`, the WP_REST_Request for the settings rows
         */
        return Capability::filtered("alpaca_bot/capability/{$hook}", $stored, ...$args);
    }

    /** Whether code moved this row off what the site saved, which is what the Access tab shows as "set in code". */
    public function overridden(string $row, mixed ...$args): bool
    {
        return $this->effective($row, ...$args) !== $this->stored($row);
    }

    /**
     * Whether `$userId` passes `$row`. user_can() of that id rather than current_user_can(), for
     * the reason DraftPostToolkit gives: the id that is checked is the id the work is done as (the
     * turn's user, a shortcode's viewer), so the check and the act cannot disagree about who is
     * asking.
     */
    public function allows(int $userId, string $row, mixed ...$args): bool
    {
        return (bool) user_can($userId, $this->effective($row, ...$args));
    }

    /** A row's hook segment: dots as slashes, so `tool.web_fetch` is `tool/web_fetch` and `shortcode` is itself. */
    private static function hook(string $row): string
    {
        return str_replace('.', '/', $row);
    }
}
```

and the two helpers next to `sanitizeUrl()`:

```php
    /**
     * One Settings › Access row: a select over Access::CAPABILITIES, starting at that row's
     * Access::defaults() value. `select` is what validates it — coerce() keeps only a value that is
     * a key of `options`, so a PUT or a hand-edited option naming anything else stores the default.
     *
     * @return Field
     */
    private static function access(string $row, string $label, string $description): array
    {
        return ['type' => 'select', 'default' => Access::defaults()[$row], 'section' => 'access', 'label' => $label, 'description' => $description, 'options' => self::capabilityLabels()];
    }

    /**
     * Access::CAPABILITIES with the words an operator picks from. The roles named are the stock
     * ones that hold each capability; a site with custom roles gets whoever holds it there, which
     * is why the row stores the capability and not a role.
     *
     * @return array<string, string>
     */
    private static function capabilityLabels(): array
    {
        return [
            'manage_options' => __('Administrators', 'alpaca-bot'),
            'edit_others_posts' => __('Editors and up', 'alpaca-bot'),
            'publish_posts' => __('Authors and up', 'alpaca-bot'),
            'edit_posts' => __('Contributors and up', 'alpaca-bot'),
            'read' => __('Any logged-in user', 'alpaca-bot'),
        ];
    }
```

### Task 7: The Chat row feeds the menu and every chat route's capability default

**Files:**
- Modify: `src/Rest/Controller.php` (route shape docblock :29-35, `register()` :37-51, `permission()` :53-92 — the filter docblock moves into a new `capability()`)
- Modify: `src/Rest/ChatController.php:48`, `src/Rest/StreamController.php:78`, `src/Rest/ConversationsController.php:36-39`, `src/Rest/ModelsController.php:58`, `src/Rest/UsageController.php:35`, `src/Rest/ViewController.php:66-71` (the `'capability' => 'edit_posts'` cells)
- Modify: `src/Admin/Menu.php` (class docblock :14-18, constructor :75, `register()` :79-91)
- Modify: `src/Plugin.php` (`controllers()` :232-258 and its filter docblock :234-244; the `Admin\Menu` construction :182)
- Modify: `src/Rest/ViewController.php:24`, `src/Rest/ModelsController.php:23`, `src/Abilities/Register.php:44-46` (sentences this falsifies)
- Modify: `docs/api.md` (:99-125 the capability table and its intro, :143-160 the route table, :785-791 the admin surface), `README.md:88` and `README.md:183`
- Regenerate: `docs/hooks.md`, `readme.txt` (`composer docs:readme`)
- Test: `tests/Unit/Rest/ControllerTest.php`, `tests/Unit/Admin/MenuTest.php`, the six controller route tests (`ChatControllerTest.php:33`, `StreamControllerTest.php:43`, `ConversationsControllerTest.php:29`, `ModelsControllerTest.php:32`, `UsageControllerTest.php:27`, `ViewControllerTest.php:73`), `tests/Integration/ChatRoutesTest.php`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Access::stored('chat')`, `Access::defaults()` (Task 6).
- Produces: `Rest\Controller::CHAT` (the string `'chat'`, the token a route declares instead of a capability name); `Rest\Controller::useAccess(Access $access): void`; `protected Rest\Controller::capability(string $route, string $declared, \WP_REST_Request $request): string`, which Task 9 overrides; `Admin\Menu::__construct(SettingsPage $settings, callable $chatRenderer, Access $access)`.

**Which way the value reaches the controllers, and why this way.** Two candidates: inject `Access` into each of the six constructors, or let the base resolve a row token. Injection is the bigger diff (six constructors, six `new` sites in `Plugin::controllers()`, and every test that builds a controller — `tests/Unit/Rest/*Test.php` build them directly) and it forces the row to be read in `routes()`, which runs on `rest_api_init` for *every* REST request on the site, including `/wp/v2/*`. The token is one cell per route, one setter on the base, and the row is read inside the permission callback — so only a request that actually reaches one of our routes reads it, and it reads through the memoised `Store`. Token wins; `useAccess()` is called once, in `Plugin::controllers()`, on everything the `alpaca_bot/rest/controllers` filter hands back, so a third party's subclass can declare `'capability' => Controller::CHAT` too.

`src/Rest/Controller.php`:

3. `private ?Access $access = null;` with `useAccess()`:

```php
    /**
     * The Settings › Access rows this controller's routes resolve tokens against.
     * Plugin::controllers() hands every controller the container's one Access — a third party's
     * subclass included, after the `alpaca_bot/rest/controllers` filter — so a route may declare
     * self::CHAT wherever it is registered from. A controller nobody handed one (a test, a site
     * registering one by hand) resolves the token to Access::defaults(), never to the literal.
     *
     * @since 0.6.0
     */
    public function useAccess(Access $access): void
    {
        $this->access = $access;
    }
```

4. `permission()` keeps its signature and its refusal, and delegates the resolution, so a subclass can put another chain in front of it (Task 9 does). The closure stops being `static` because it now calls `$this->capability()`:

```php
    public function permission(string $route, string $capability): \Closure
    {
        return function (\WP_REST_Request $request) use ($route, $capability): bool|\WP_Error {
            return current_user_can($this->capability($route, $capability, $request)) ? true : Errors::forbidden();
        };
    }

    /**
     * The capability this route's permission callback checks for this request: the route's
     * declared capability — or, for self::CHAT, the Chat row of Settings › Access — through the
     * route's own filter. Overridable, which is how Rest\SettingsController puts its read/write
     * rows and the 0.5 settings key in front of it.
     */
    protected function capability(string $route, string $declared, \WP_REST_Request $request): string
    {
        $default = $declared === self::CHAT
            ? ($this->access?->stored(self::CHAT) ?? Access::defaults()[self::CHAT])
            : $declared;
        /**
         * … the existing filter docblock from :66-88, with its first @param rewritten to:
         * @param string           $capability the route's default: its declared capability, or for a chat route the Chat row of Settings › Access (`edit_posts` unless the site changed it)
         * @param \WP_REST_Request $request    the request being authorised
         */
        return Capability::filtered("alpaca_bot/capability/{$route}", $default, $request);
    }
```

5. The route-shape docblock at :29-35 gains a sentence: *"`capability` is a capability name, or `Controller::CHAT` for a route that follows the Chat row of Settings › Access."*

The six controllers: `'capability' => 'edit_posts'` becomes `'capability' => self::CHAT` (`ChatController.php:48`, `StreamController.php:78`, `ModelsController.php:58`, `UsageController.php:35`, the four `ConversationsController.php:36-39` cells and the six `ViewController.php:66-71` cells). **Files:**
- Modify: `src/Toolkit/Registry.php` (class docblock :11-38, `__construct()` :44, `register()` :46-49, `enabled()` :57-96 with the `alpaca_bot/toolkits` docblock at :68-87)
- Modify: `src/Plugin.php:124` (the registry construction)

- Modify: `src/Abilities/Register.php` (:71-75, :316-317, the refusal at :325-330), `src/Shortcodes/AgentShim.php` (:26-31, the refusal at :105-106), `src/Rest/ModelsController.php:21-23`
- Modify: `src/Admin/HelpTabs.php` (method docblock :117-128, the tools tab's "Who can reach it" paragraph :142-147 and its "Opening the chat…" paragraph :148-155)
- Modify: `bin/hooks-doc.php` (:526-542, the hand-written "Before you loosen a capability filter" prose), `README.md:106` and `README.md:110-119`, `README.md:183`
- Regenerate: `docs/hooks.md`, `readme.txt`
- Test: `tests/Unit/Toolkit/RegistryTest.php`, `tests/Pest.php` (`registryWith()` :556-563, `shortcodeViewer()` :698-703), `tests/Unit/Shortcodes/AgentShimTest.php` (`agentShim()` :34-40 and :117-130), `tests/Unit/Abilities/RegisterTest.php`, `tests/Unit/Admin/HelpTabsTest.php:111-125`, `tests/Integration/ToolkitsTest.php`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Access::allows(int $userId, string $row, mixed ...$args): bool` (Task 6).
- Produces: `Toolkit\Registry::__construct(Store $store, ?Access $access = null)` and `Toolkit\Registry::register(string $id, ToolkitInterface $toolkit, ?string $row = null): void` — the row defaults to `'tool.' . $id`, and Task 27 registers each MCP toolkit with `'mcp.' . $id`.

**Every path the floor has to cover.** `enabled()` has exactly three callers in `src/`, verified by `grep -rn "enabled(" src` and reading each: `Chat\Pipeline::toolkitsFor()` (`src/Chat/Pipeline.php:936`, every model turn), `Abilities\Register::toolkit()` (`src/Abilities/Register.php:321`, every ability call including an MCP client's), and `Shortcodes\AgentShim::fetch()` (`src/Shortcodes/AgentShim.php:104`, the `[alpacabot_agent name="get"]` fetch). `Registry::tool()` (:103) is a static over a toolkit it is handed, so it is downstream of all three. `src/Cli/ChatCommand.php:183` names `enabled()` in a docblock only — the CLI's model listing takes no user and calls nothing. Putting the floor inside `enabled()` therefore covers the three, and no caller has to remember it.

`src/Toolkit/Registry.php`:

```php
    /** @var array<string, ToolkitInterface> in registration order; a second register() of an id replaces in place */
    private array $toolkits = [];

    /** @var array<string, string> id => the Settings › Access row that gates it */
    private array $rows = [];

    private Access $access;

    public function __construct(private Store $store, ?Access $access = null)
    {
        $this->access = $access ?? new Access($store);
    }

    /**
     * `$row` is the Settings › Access row enabled() holds this toolkit to, `tool.{id}` unless a
     * caller names another (Task 27's MCP toolkits are `mcp.{id}`). It is the registrar's, not the
     * id's, so a toolkit registered under a display id is still gated by the row an administrator
     * sees.
     */
    public function register(string $id, ToolkitInterface $toolkit, ?string $row = null): void
    {
        $this->toolkits[$id] = $toolkit;
        $this->rows[$id] = $row ?? 'tool.' . $id;
    }
```

and in `enabled()`, the subset line becomes:

```php
        $subset = array_filter(
            $this->toolkits,
            fn(string $id): bool => in_array($id, $enabled, true) && $this->access->allows($userId, $this->rows[$id], $userId),
            ARRAY_FILTER_USE_KEY,
        );
```

The setting is tested first so a switched-off toolkit costs no capability lookup, and the whole floor runs before `apply_filters('alpaca_bot/toolkits', …)` at :88, which is unchanged.

Docblock rewrites (this is the task's other half):

- `src/Toolkit/Registry.php:14-21` — "Two gates, in order." becomes **three**: "Three gates, in order. The `toolkits.enabled` setting names the built-ins an administrator has switched on; it is a checkbox list over the ids Schema knows, so it can never name a toolkit the plugin did not ship (Schema::coerce() drops anything else). Then the floor: a toolkit is kept only for a user who passes its Settings › Access row (Access::allows(), `tool.{id}` unless register() was given another), asked with `user_can()` of the id the turn runs as rather than `current_user_can()`, for the reason DraftPostToolkit gives — the id that is checked is the id the tool would act as, so the check and the act cannot disagree about who is asking. Then filter `alpaca_bot/toolkits` … runs over what is left, and it is the one extension point … The floor runs before the filter on purpose: the filter can still hand a toolkit back for a user, which is how a site gives a tool to a role no row covers, and it can still take one away."
- `src/Toolkit/Registry.php:76-81` — the paragraph beginning "This filter is also, today, the only capability gate over the tools … A floor of enabled()'s own is a later 0.x release." is deleted and replaced by: "It is no longer the only capability gate over the tools: enabled() has already dropped every toolkit whose Settings › Access row this user fails (`alpaca_bot/capability/tool/{id}`, `edit_posts` for the built-ins), so a site that opens `alpaca_bot/capability/chat` to a role hands that role the chat and no tools until a row admits them. What this filter is, is the last word: a toolkit added here has no row and no Settings entry, so `user_can($userId, …)` here is the site's own gate over it, and a toolkit taken away here is gone whatever its row says."
- `src/Rest/Controller.php` (the tools paragraph inside `capability()` after Task 7) — "Opening `chat` or `chat/stream` opens every enabled tool to that role as well … has no capability check of its own, so there is nothing between 'may chat' and 'may call the tools that are switched on'" becomes: "Opening `chat` or `chat/stream` does not open the tools with it. Toolkit\Registry::enabled() offers a turn only the toolkits whose Settings › Access row the turn's user passes (`alpaca_bot/capability/tool/{id}`), before `alpaca_bot/toolkits` runs, so a role admitted here only to converse gets no tools until a row admits it — `web_fetch`, which makes the web server send an outbound request and hands the reply back, included. `draft_post` re-checks a capability of its own on top. `read` and `exist` are honoured strings, so `read` is every Subscriber and `exist` is every visitor, logged out included. ### Task 9: Settings read/write capability split (Kanboard #4332)

**Files:**
- Modify: `src/Rest/SettingsController.php` (class docblock :27-35 and :66-75, `routes()` :81-88, a new `capability()` override; `show()`'s `reveal` check at :92 is untouched)
- Modify: `src/Access.php` (`effective()` — the settings rows chain the 0.5 key first)
- Modify: `bin/hooks-doc.php` (:544-549, the hand-written `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` paragraph)
- Modify: `docs/api.md` (:103-125 the filter-key table and the paragraph under it, :151-153 the route table, :366-368)
- Regenerate: `docs/hooks.md`
- Test: `tests/Unit/Rest/SettingsControllerTest.php` (:31-41 pins the declared capabilities), `tests/Unit/AccessTest.php`, `tests/Unit/HooksDocTest.php`, `tests/Integration/SettingsRoutesTest.php`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Access::effective(string $row, mixed ...$args)` (Task 6), `Rest\Controller::capability()` and `useAccess()` (Task 7).
- Produces: the hooks `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/read` and `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/write` (through Access's `{hook}` call site), each defaulting to whatever `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` returns over the `settings.read` / `settings.write` row.

**The route keys today, verified.** `Controller::routeKey()` (`src/Rest/Controller.php:100-103`) gives `/settings` → `settings` for **both** verbs (the GET at `SettingsController.php:84` and the PUT at :85 share the path, so `PUT /settings` is authorised by `alpaca_bot/capability/settings`, which is exactly the hazard :71-75 warns about), and `/settings/schema` → `settings/schema`. After this task the three routes resolve through `Access::effective()` instead of the base's `{route}` filter: the 0.5 key `settings` is applied to all three, and `settings/schema` — 0.5's own key for the schema route — is retired, folded into `settings/read`. That follows the spec's "both default to the value of the existing `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` key"; it is the one back-compat break in the task, it can only widen the schema read back to the row (never narrow it), and Task 29's upgrade notice says so.

```php
    public function test_the_read_row_can_admit_an_editor_without_admitting_the_write_or_the_key(): void
```

`src/Access.php`, inside `effective()` between `$stored` and the `{hook}` call:

```php
        if (str_starts_with($row, 'settings.')) {
            /**
             * Filters the capability both settings rows start from: 0.5's one key for the settings
             * routes, kept as the default the newer keys receive so a site that filtered it keeps
             * what it set. It runs over the stored row, and `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/read` or
             * `…/settings/write` runs over what it returns, so the newer key wins where both are
             * set. Since 0.6 it covers `GET /settings/schema` too, which 0.5 filtered under its own
             * `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/schema` key; that key is no longer applied. One key
             * over both verbs is why the rows exist: tighten the write with `…/settings/write`
             * rather than here, or off the WP_REST_Request's `get_method()`.
             *
             * @since 0.5.0
             * @param string $capability the `settings.read` or `settings.write` row, `manage_options` by default
             * @param mixed  ...$args    the WP_REST_Request being authorised
             */
            $stored = Capability::filtered('alpaca_bot/capability/settings', $stored, ...$args);
        }
```

`src/Rest/SettingsController.php` — `use AlpacaBot\Access;`, the row tokens in `routes()`:

```php
            ['path' => '/settings', 'methods' => 'GET', 'callback' => [$this, 'show'], 'capability' => 'settings.read', 'args' => ['reveal' => ['type' => 'boolean', 'default' => false]]],
            ['path' => '/settings', 'methods' => 'PUT', 'callback' => [$this, 'update'], 'capability' => 'settings.write'],
            ['path' => '/settings/schema', 'methods' => 'GET', 'callback' => [$this, 'schema'], 'capability' => 'settings.read'],
```

and the override:

```php
    /**
     * These three routes resolve their row rather than the base's `alpaca_bot/capability/{route}`:
     * Access::effective() applies 0.5's `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` over the stored row and
     * then the row's own key, which is the whole of the split. `$declared` is the row, not a
     * capability, so the verb is read off the route table rather than off the request — the GET
     * and the PUT share a path and would otherwise be one key again.
     *
     * The Access is the container's (Controller::useAccess()); one built over this controller's own
     * Store stands in for a controller nobody handed one, and shares that Store's single read.
     */
    protected function capability(string $route, string $declared, \WP_REST_Request $request): string
    {
        return ($this->access ?? new Access($this->store))->effective($declared, $request);
    }
```

(`$this->access` is the base's property from Task 7; give it `protected` visibility there, or add a `protected function access(): ?Access` accessor and call that — either is fine, but say which in the report.)

Docblock rewrites:
- `src/Rest/SettingsController.php:27-35` — "The gate is filtered — `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` (below) — and the filter decides the route … the write verb is the other half of that argument and is a 0.6 ticket (splitting the filter into read and write keys), not a check that can be added here without deciding what a 'read-only settings' role means." becomes: "The gate is the `settings.read` row of Settings › Access and its filters (capability() below), and a site may lower that row or filter it for a custom role, so without this check it would be handing that role the provider credential in cleartext. A caller the gate admitted without `manage_options` is not refused the route, only the secret: the reply is the masked read, the same one they get without the flag. Nothing else on the route has a floor, because nothing else on it is a credential — and the write, the other half of that argument in 0.5, now has a row and a key of its own, so admitting a role to the read no longer admits it to the PUT."
- `src/Rest/SettingsController.php:66-75` — the two paragraphs about `routeKey()` and "One key over both verbs" become: "Capability: the two GETs ask the `settings.read` row and the PUT the `settings.write` row, both `manage_options` by default, through Access::effective() — 0.5's `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` over the stored row, then `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/read` or `…/settings/write` over what it returned, so a site that filtered the old key keeps what it set and the newer key wins where both are set. These routes do not apply `alpaca_bot/capability/{route}`, so 0.5's `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/schema` is gone, folded into `settings/read`: a site that had named it to *open* the schema had to open `settings` too and is unaffected; one that had named it only to tighten the schema names `settings/read` instead. Splitting the verbs is what makes a read-only settings role possible: `provider.base_url` is a settable field, so a role admitted to the write can point every turn the site takes at a server of its choosing."
- `bin/hooks-doc.php:544-549` — the `alpaca_bot/capability/settings` paragraph becomes: "`alpaca_bot/capability/settings` is the other one worth a second look. It is 0.5's one key over both verbs on `/settings`, and it is still applied — as the *default* the newer `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/read` and `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/write` receive, over the Settings › Access rows of the same names. So admitting a role through the old key still admits it to the write; name `…/settings/write` to take that back. `alpaca_bot/capability/settings/schema` is no longer applied: the schema route asks `…/settings/read`. The `?reveal=1` parameter, which answers with the provider API key in cleartext, asks `manage_options` on its own account and is not reachable through any of them."
- `docs/api.md:103-125` — the `settings` and `settings/schema` rows of the filter-key table become two rows, `settings/read` (`GET /settings`, `GET /settings/schema`) and `settings/write` (`PUT /settings`), each "the row of that name (`manage_options`), after `alpaca_bot/capability/settings`"; the paragraph beginning "The filter is `alpaca_bot/capability/{key}` … `settings/schema` is its own key: loosening `settings` for a custom role does not let that role read the schema until you name it too." is replaced by one that says the settings routes are the exception to `{route}` keys, gives the chain, and says `settings/schema` is retired.
- `docs/api.md:151-153` — the capability column becomes `settings.read` row, `settings.write` row, `settings.read` row.
- `docs/api.md:366-368` — "Administrators only." → "Administrators by default: the two GETs ask the `settings.read` row of Settings › Access and the PUT asks `settings.write` (section 2). `?reveal=1` needs `manage_options` whatever the rows say."

```php
    public function __construct(
```

`CAPABILITY` (:106-107) is deleted. `viewerMayGenerate()` stops being static and takes what the filter is given:

```php
    /**
     * Whether this viewer may make the page spend: logged in, and past the Shortcode row of
     * Settings › Access for the post and tag being rendered. The login check stays in front of the
     * row because `exist` is an honoured capability name and user_can(0, 'exist') is true — a
     * visitor must never trigger a generation, whatever a row or a filter says.
     */
    private function viewerMayGenerate(int $postId, string $tag): bool
    {
        return is_user_logged_in() && $this->access->allows((int) get_current_user_id(), 'shortcode', $postId, $tag);
    }
```

### Task 11: A distinct stream-concurrency 429, and the composer shows the reason (Kanboard #4333)

**Files:**
- Modify: `src/Errors.php` (the file Task 1 moved `Rest\Errors` to; `tooMany()` at :34-41 is untouched, a sibling joins it)
- Modify: `src/Rest/StreamBudget.php` (`claim()` :198-248 — its `@return`, both returns, and the `alpaca_bot/stream/concurrent` docblock :216-228; class docblock :68)
- Modify: `src/Rest/StreamController.php:147-152` (the refusal)
- Modify: `resources/ts/chat.ts` (`RestError` :21, `refused()` :64-74)
- Modify: `docs/api.md` (:269 the stream route's refusal table, :685 the error table, :736-739 the rate-limit prose)
- Regenerate: `docs/hooks.md` (the `alpaca_bot/stream/concurrent` row)
- Test: `tests/Unit/Rest/StreamBudgetTest.php`, `tests/Unit/Rest/StreamControllerTest.php:514-541`, `tests/e2e/composer.spec.ts` (header :29-33, the first test :106-126, one new case)

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `StreamBudget::claim(int $userId)`.
- Produces: `Errors::streamConcurrency(int $limit, int $retryAfter): \WP_Error` — code `alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency`, status 429, data `retry_after` and `limit`; `claim()` returns `array{slot: string|null, retry_after: int, limit: int}`.

**How the client sees it, verified.** The redemption is not an `EventSource` — `resources/ts/stream.ts:1-6` says why (EventSource cannot send `X-WP-Nonce`), so `chat.ts:145` redeems with `fetch()` and `:146` is the 0.5 P5 data-loss fix: anything whose `content-type` is not `text/event-stream` goes to `refused(stream.status, await restError(stream))`, which reads the JSON body. So the reason is already readable; what 0.5 could not do is tell the two 429s apart, and `refused()` (:65-74) shows every non-nonce refusal as `notice('error', …)`. This task gives the body its own code and `limit`, and makes the composer say it as a *warning* — the ticket is unspent and the turn is still sendable, which an error-red banner denies. `tests/e2e/composer.spec.ts` is the coverage for that path (chat.ts exports nothing until Task 15), so the new case goes there.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**

```php
    $over = $controller->handle(restRequest('GET', '/alpaca-bot/v1/chat/42/stream', ['id' => '42', 'token' => 'tok4']));
    expect($over)->toBeInstanceOf(WP_REST_Response::class)
        ->and($over->get_status())->toBe(429)
        // Its own code, not the per-minute bucket's: the two 429s mean different waits, and the

        ->and($over->get_data()['message'])->toBe('You already have 3 streams open. Wait for one to finish, then send again.')
        ->and($over->get_data()['data'])->toBe(['status' => 429, 'retry_after' => 720, 'limit' => 3])
        // The soonest of this person's leases lapses then, so waiting it out gets through.
        ->and($over->get_headers())->toBe(['Retry-After' => '720'])
        // Unspent: the ticket is still there to redeem when a slot frees.
        ->and($h->transients)->toHaveKey(ChatController::STREAM_TRANSIENT . 'tok4')
```

`tests/e2e/composer.spec.ts` — the first test (:106-126) stops standing in for the concurrency case, because the server cannot answer that redemption with `alpaca_bot_rate_limited` any more. It keeps its job (a redemption that is not an event stream) with a refusal that is still real, and the concurrency case becomes its own test:

```ts
test('a stream redemption refused as forbidden gives the message and the image back and leaves no turn in the transcript', async ({ page }) => {
  const seen = await shell(page, {
    status: 403,
    contentType: 'application/json',
    body: JSON.stringify({ code: 'rest_forbidden', message: 'Invalid or expired stream token.', data: { status: 403 } }),
  });

  await send(page, 'the message that must survive');

  await expect(page.locator('#ab-status')).toContainText('Invalid or expired stream token');
  await expect(page.locator('#ab-message')).toHaveValue('the message that must survive');
  await expect(page.locator('#ab-form input[name="images"]')).toHaveValue(IMAGE);
  await expect(page.locator('#ab-messages article')).toHaveCount(0);
  expect(seen.filter((p) => p.startsWith('/wp-json/alpaca-bot/v1/chat/'))).toHaveLength(1);
  await expect(page.locator('[data-action="send"]')).toBeEnabled();
});

test('a redemption refused for concurrency says how many streams are open, as a warning, and keeps the message for the retry', async ({ page }) => {
  // StreamBudget's own 429: the ticket is not spent and lives 120 s, so this is "not yet", not a
  // failure — the composer keeps what was typed and says why rather than showing a generic error.
  const seen = await shell(page, {
    status: 429,
    contentType: 'application/json',
    body: JSON.stringify({
      code: 'alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency',
      message: 'You already have 3 streams open. Wait for one to finish, then send again.',
      data: { status: 429, retry_after: 720, limit: 3 },
    }),
  });

  await send(page, 'the message that waits its turn');

  await expect(page.locator('#ab-status .notice-warning')).toContainText('You already have 3 streams open');
  await expect(page.locator('#ab-message')).toHaveValue('the message that waits its turn');
  await expect(page.locator('#ab-form input[name="images"]')).toHaveValue(IMAGE);
  await expect(page.locator('#ab-messages article')).toHaveCount(0);
  expect(seen.filter((p) => p.startsWith('/wp-json/alpaca-bot/v1/chat/'))).toHaveLength(1);
  await expect(page.locator('[data-action="send"]')).toBeEnabled();
});
```

Expected: FAIL — `claim()` returns two keys (`src/Rest/StreamBudget.php:211-247`), so `['limit' => 3]` is missing; the refusal is `Errors::tooMany()` (`StreamController.php:149`), so the code is `alpaca_bot_rate_limited` and there is no `limit`; and the Playwright case fails on `#ab-status .notice-warning` because `refused()` renders every refusal as `notice-error` (`resources/ts/chat.ts:73`).

- [ ] **Step 3: Implement**

`src/Errors.php`, next to `tooMany()`:

```php
    /**
     * 429 for a stream redemption that found this person already holding as many live streams as
     * the site allows (StreamBudget::claim()). Its own code rather than tooMany()'s, because the
     * two 429s are different waits: the per-minute bucket turns over within the minute and the
     * request was spent, while this one frees when one of the person's own streams ends and the
     * ticket is left unspent for the retry. A client that keys on the code can say so; one that
     * keys on the status alone still sees a 429 with Retry-After. The message names the number
     * because that is the thing the person can act on, and `limit` carries it for a client that
     * words its own.
     *
     * @since 0.6.0
     */
    public static function streamConcurrency(int $limit, int $retryAfter): \WP_Error
    {
        return new \WP_Error(
            'alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency',
            sprintf(
                /* translators: %d: how many replies one person may have streaming at once */
                _n(
                    'You already have %d stream open. Wait for it to finish, then send again.',
                    'You already have %d streams open. Wait for one to finish, then send again.',
                    $limit,
                    'alpaca-bot',
                ),
                $limit,
            ),
            ['status' => 429, 'retry_after' => $retryAfter, 'limit' => $limit],
        );
    }
```

`src/Rest/StreamBudget.php` — `claim()`'s `@return` becomes `array{slot: string|null, retry_after: int, limit: int}` with a line added to the docblock: *"`limit` is the number this claim was held to, after `alpaca_bot/stream/concurrent`, so a refusal can say how many streams the person has rather than quoting the shipped LIMIT at a site that moved it."* Both returns carry it:

```php
                return ['slot' => $index . ':' . $held, 'retry_after' => 0, 'limit' => $limit];
...
        return ['slot' => null, 'retry_after' => max(1, ($waits === [] ? $expires : min($waits)) - $now), 'limit' => $limit];
```

`src/Rest/StreamController.php:149`:

```php
            $response = rest_convert_error_to_response(Errors::streamConcurrency($claim['limit'], $claim['retry_after']));
```

`resources/ts/chat.ts`:

```ts
interface RestError { code?: string; message?: string; data?: { limit?: number; retry_after?: number } }
```

and in `refused()`, before the final `notice('error', …)`:

```ts
    // The concurrency refusal is "not yet", not a failure: the ticket is unspent and send()'s
    // finally has already given the message and the image back, so this says why and stays out
    // of the error colour. The server's message names how many streams are open (data.limit).
    if (error.code === 'alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency') {
      notice('warning', error.message || t('failed'));
      return;
    }
```

(`notice()`'s third argument is an icon name and is deliberately not used here: `connectivity()` owns `wifi-off`, and a wait has no icon of its own.)

Docs and docblocks:
- `src/Rest/StreamBudget.php:68` — "a refused redemption is a 429 that leaves the ticket unspent" → "a refused redemption is a 429 `alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency` that leaves the ticket unspent".
- `src/Rest/StreamBudget.php:219-221` (the `alpaca_bot/stream/concurrent` docblock) — "a redemption over the limit is a 429 and its ticket stays valid" → "a redemption over the limit is a 429 `alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency`, whose message and `limit` both carry whatever this filter returned, and its ticket stays valid".
- `docs/api.md:269` — the row's answer becomes "429 `alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency`, `Retry-After` header, `data.retry_after` in seconds and `data.limit` (how many streams one person may hold). **The ticket is not spent**: retry it while it lives. The wait is a ceiling — the soonest running stream's lease — so it can be longer than the 120 s the ticket has left, and then the turn has to be posted again".
- `docs/api.md:685` — the one 429 row splits in two: `alpaca_bot_rate_limited` keeps "the `chat` bucket is spent for this minute (`Retry-After` holds the seconds until it turns over)" with data `retry_after`; a new row, `429 alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency`, reads "A stream redemption found this user already at the concurrent-stream cap. `Retry-After` is a ceiling on the wait for a slot — up to the site's whole stream budget, 720 s at the defaults — and the ticket is left unspent" with data `retry_after`, `limit`.
- `docs/api.md:736-739` — "a redemption over that is the 429 in the table above with its ticket left unspent" → "a redemption over that is a 429 `alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency` with its ticket left unspent, which is a different code from the per-minute bucket's so a client can tell "wait for the minute" from "wait for your own stream to finish"".
- `tests/e2e/composer.spec.ts:29-33` — "The last one is the one this file was written for: a stream redemption that comes back as something other than text/event-stream (StreamBudget's concurrency 429, an expired or replayed ticket) used to remove the empty assistant bubble and return…" keeps its history and gains the split: "…Both refusals of that shape are covered below: an expired or replayed ticket (403) and the concurrency cap (429 `alpaca_bot_stream_concurrency`), which is the one refusal the composer shows as a warning rather than an error, because the ticket survives it."

### Task 12: web_fetch: resolve once, check, and pin into cURL on every hop (Kanboard #4330)

**What is verified in WordPress 7.1 core** (read in the `wph-alpaca10-wordpress-1` container, `/var/www/html`, `$wp_version = '7.1'`):

| Claim | Where |
| --- | --- |
| `http_api_curl` fires for the Requests cURL transport. `WP_HTTP_Requests_Hooks::dispatch()` re-fires Requests' `curl.before_send` as `do_action_ref_array( 'http_api_curl', array( &$parameters[0], $this->request, $this->url ) )`: the cURL handle, the parsed request args, and the URL. | `wp-includes/class-wp-http-requests-hooks.php:56-58` |
| It fires after the handle is fully set up and before `curl_exec()`: `setup_handle()` (which sets `CURLOPT_URL` at `:447`) runs, then `dispatch('curl.before_send', [&$this->handle])`. | `wp-includes/Requests/src/Transport/Curl.php:172-174`, `:205` |
| Every request gets a fresh handle: `get_transport()` returns `new $class()` and Curl's constructor calls `curl_init()`, so a `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` entry cannot outlive its request. | `Requests/src/Requests.php:268-275`, `Transport/Curl.php:102-105` |
| `redirection => 0` means Requests follows nothing, and a 3xx comes back as a response rather than a "Too many redirects" exception: `if ( empty( $parsed_args['redirection'] ) ) { $options['follow_redirects'] = false; }`. Requests throws `toomanyredirects` only inside `follow_redirects === true`. | `wp-includes/class-wp-http.php:359-363`; `Requests.php:787-813` |
| Core's own redirect validation (`validate_redirects` on `requests.before_redirect`) is registered, but no longer runs because Requests no longer follows. The toolkit's re-check per hop replaces it. | `class-wp-http.php:349-354`, `:529-533` |
| `wp_safe_remote_get()` still runs `wp_http_validate_url()` inside the request, which does its own DNS lookup. That lookup can only refuse; the pinned connection never uses its answer. | `wp-includes/http.php:81-85`; `class-wp-http.php:283-289` |
| A proxy configured for the HTTP API (`WP_PROXY_HOST`/`WP_PROXY_PORT`) becomes a Requests `Proxy\Http`, which sets `CURLOPT_PROXY` on the same `curl.before_send` hook. The name then goes to the proxy, which resolves it itself, so a `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` entry for the target host is not used. | `class-wp-http.php:401-410`; `Requests/src/Proxy/Http.php:99`, `:114`; `class-wp-http-proxy.php:57-59` |
| HTTPS keeps verifying against the hostname. Requests changes `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` only to turn it off when `sslverify` is false (`:194-202`), so libcurl's default (VERIFYHOST 2) checks the certificate against the host in `CURLOPT_URL`. `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` only seeds the handle's DNS cache for `host:port`, so SNI, the Host header and certificate verification all keep using the URL's host. That is libcurl's documented `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` behaviour, and why the pin is an address and not a rewritten URL. Container libcurl: 8.14.1, SSL on. | `Transport/Curl.php:194-202` |
| The legacy `WP_Http_Curl` also fires `http_api_curl` (`class-wp-http-curl.php:236`), but `WP_Http::request()` never uses it; it goes through `WpOrg\Requests\Requests::request()` (`class-wp-http.php:416`). | as cited |

**Design:**
- `Toolkit\AddressPin::resolve()` becomes the one address rule: one lookup, both families, every answer checked, and one address returned. The lookup moves there too as `AddressPin::lookup()`, the body of today's `WebFetchToolkit::resolve()`.
- `WebFetchToolkit::fetch()` follows redirects itself, up to `MAX_REDIRECTS` (3). Each hop is `wp_http_validate_url()`, then `pin()`, then `wp_safe_remote_get()` with `redirection => 0` and one `CurlPin` hooked on `http_api_curl` for exactly that call and unhooked in a `finally`.
- `CurlPin` applies `host:port:ip` to whatever handle the hook hands it. This is safe unconditionally: a `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` entry changes nothing for a request to any other `host:port`, and it pins a same-host request to the checked address. So no URL matching is needed, and URL matching would be fragile, since core rewrites the URL (`wp_kses_bad_protocol()`, `class-wp-http.php:287-289`) before building the hooks object.
- The site's own host keeps its exemption and is neither looked up nor pinned, as today (`WebFetchToolkit.php:192`). An address literal needs no pin.

**Files:**
- Create: `src/Toolkit/AddressPin.php`, `src/Toolkit/AddressRefused.php`, `src/Toolkit/CurlPin.php`
- Modify: `src/Toolkit/WebFetchToolkit.php`:
  - class docblock `:17-48`;
  - `fetch()` `:118-173`;
  - `hostIsPublic()` `:175-210`, replaced by `pin()`;
  - `resolve()` `:212-255`, moved to `AddressPin::lookup()`;
  - new `MAX_REDIRECTS` const and `get()`/`redirect()` privates.
- Modify: `src/Toolkit/SpecialPurposeAddress.php` docblock `:20-24`
- Modify: `tests/stubs/wp-requests.php` docblock `:3-10`
- Test: `tests/Unit/Toolkit/AddressPinTest.php` (new), `tests/Unit/Toolkit/CurlPinTest.php` (new), `tests/Unit/Toolkit/WebFetchToolkitTest.php`, `tests/Integration/ToolkitsTest.php`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Toolkit\SpecialPurposeAddress::isAddress(string): bool`, `::match(string): ?string` (`SpecialPurposeAddress.php:70-93`).
- Produces:
  - `final class Toolkit\AddressRefused extends \RuntimeException`
  - `final class Toolkit\AddressPin`:
    - `public static function resolve(string $host, string $url = '', ?\Closure $lookup = null): string` returns the checked address to connect to, or throws `AddressRefused`. The sheet's name and required parameter are kept; the two optional trailing parameters are explained under Drafting notes.
    - `public static function lookup(string $host, ?\Closure $a = null, ?\Closure $aaaa = null): array` returns `list<string>`.
  - `final class Toolkit\CurlPin`: `public readonly string $entry`; `public function __construct(string $host, int $port, string $ip, ?\Closure $setopt = null)`; `public function __invoke(mixed $handle): void`.
  - `WebFetchToolkit::MAX_REDIRECTS = 3`. `WebFetchToolkit::resolve()` is removed; its only callers are tests.

`tests/Unit/Toolkit/AddressPinTest.php`:
```php
<?php

declare(strict_types=1);

use AlpacaBot\Toolkit\AddressPin;
use AlpacaBot\Toolkit\AddressRefused;
use Brain\Monkey\Filters;

// AddressPin is the one address rule both transports share: web_fetch (cURL, through
// http_api_curl) and the MCP egress client (Symfony's `resolve`). It resolves a name once, holds
// every answer to SpecialPurposeAddress, and hands back the single address the connection is
// then pinned to. The lookup is injected, so nothing here touches DNS; SpecialPurposeAddressTest
// walks the table itself.

it('resolves a name once and returns its first answer, IPv4 ahead of IPv6', function (): void {
    $calls = 0;
    $ip = AddressPin::resolve('dual.test', 'https://dual.test/', static function (string $host) use (&$calls): array {
        $calls++;
        return ['93.184.216.34', '2606:2800:220:1::1'];
    });
    expect($ip)->toBe('93.184.216.34')->and($calls)->toBe(1);
    expect(AddressPin::resolve('v6only.test', '', static fn(string $host): array => ['2606:2800:220:1::1']))->toBe('2606:2800:220:1::1');
});

// The rebinding case (audit H-1): a name that answers a public address the first time and
// 127.0.0.1 the next. Resolved once, the pin keeps the first answer and no second lookup is
// made; a later hop that asks again sees the rebound answer and is refused, never pinned to it.
it('keeps the answer it checked when the name rebinds, and refuses the rebound answer if asked again', function (): void {
    $answers = [['93.184.216.34'], ['127.0.0.1']];
    $lookup = static function (string $host) use (&$answers): array {
        return array_shift($answers) ?? [];
    };
    expect(AddressPin::resolve('rebind.test', 'http://rebind.test/', $lookup))->toBe('93.184.216.34')
        ->and($answers)->toBe([['127.0.0.1']]);
    expect(static fn() => AddressPin::resolve('rebind.test', 'http://rebind.test/', $lookup))->toThrow(AddressRefused::class, 'rebind.test');
});

it('refuses a name with any special-purpose answer among public ones, a name with no answer, and a special-purpose literal', function (): void {
    $answers = [
        'mixed.test' => ['93.184.216.34', '10.0.0.7'],
        'v6.test' => ['93.184.216.34', '::1'],
        'meta.test' => ['169.254.169.254'],
        'nowhere.test' => [],
    ];
    $lookup = static fn(string $host): array => $answers[$host];
    foreach (array_keys($answers) as $host) {
        expect(static fn() => AddressPin::resolve($host, '', $lookup))->toThrow(AddressRefused::class, $host);
    }
    $never = static fn(string $host): array => throw new LogicException('a literal is not looked up');
    foreach (['127.0.0.1', '169.254.169.254', '[::1]', '[::ffff:169.254.169.254]', '100.64.0.1'] as $literal) {
        expect(static fn() => AddressPin::resolve($literal, '', $never))->toThrow(AddressRefused::class);
    }
    expect(static fn() => AddressPin::resolve('', '', $never))->toThrow(AddressRefused::class);
});

it('returns a public literal as it is, without a lookup, and lower-cases and trims a name before looking it up', function (): void {
    $never = static fn(string $host): array => throw new LogicException('a literal is not looked up');
    expect(AddressPin::resolve('93.184.216.34', '', $never))->toBe('93.184.216.34')
        ->and(AddressPin::resolve('[2606:2800:220:1::1]', '', $never))->toBe('2606:2800:220:1::1');
    $asked = [];
    AddressPin::resolve('Example.TEST.', '', static function (string $host) use (&$asked): array {
        $asked[] = $host;
        return ['93.184.216.34'];
    });
    expect($asked)->toBe(['example.test']);
});

it('lets core\'s http_request_host_is_external opt a special-purpose answer in, with the host and URL core passes', function (): void {
    Filters\expectApplied('http_request_host_is_external')->once()->with(false, 'meta.test', 'http://meta.test/')->andReturn(true);
    expect(AddressPin::resolve('meta.test', 'http://meta.test/', static fn(string $host): array => ['169.254.169.254']))->toBe('169.254.169.254');
});

// Moved from WebFetchToolkitTest with the lookup itself: a failed AAAA query is not "no AAAA
// records". dns_get_record() returns false when the query fails, and reading that as [] let a
// nameserver that answers A with a public address and drops AAAA get past the table on the A
// alone, while the transport's own lookup, which does read AAAA, chose the address.
it('looks a name up in both families, and answers [] when either lookup failed', function (): void {
    $a = static fn(string $host): array|false => ['93.184.216.34'];
    $failed = static fn(string $host): array|false => false;
    $none = static fn(string $host): array|false => [];
    $some = static fn(string $host): array|false => [['host' => $host, 'type' => 'AAAA', 'ipv6' => '2606:4700::1']];
    expect(AddressPin::lookup('dropped.test', $a, $failed))->toBe([])
        ->and(AddressPin::lookup('v4only.test', $a, $none))->toBe(['93.184.216.34'])
        ->and(AddressPin::lookup('dual.test', $a, $some))->toBe(['93.184.216.34', '2606:4700::1'])
        ->and(AddressPin::lookup('v6only.test', $failed, $some))->toBe([]);
});
```

`tests/Unit/Toolkit/CurlPinTest.php`:
```php
<?php

declare(strict_types=1);

use AlpacaBot\Toolkit\CurlPin;

// A CurlPin is the one-shot `http_api_curl` callback web_fetch hooks around a single request.
// Core hands the callback the request's cURL handle after Requests has set it up and before it
// runs (WP 7.1 class-wp-http-requests-hooks.php:56-58, Requests/src/Transport/Curl.php:172-174).
// The option is written through an injected setter so the test can read what was set; the real
// curl_setopt() is exercised once on a real handle, since cURL offers no getter for CURLOPT_RESOLVE.

it('builds the CURLOPT_RESOLVE entry cURL expects, host:port:address, an IPv6 address unbracketed as Symfony writes it', function (): void {
    expect((new CurlPin('example.test', 443, '93.184.216.34'))->entry)->toBe('example.test:443:93.184.216.34')
        ->and((new CurlPin('v6.test', 8080, '2606:2800:220:1::1'))->entry)->toBe('v6.test:8080:2606:2800:220:1::1');
});

it('sets exactly that entry as CURLOPT_RESOLVE on the handle it is handed', function (): void {
    $set = [];
    $handle = curl_init();
    (new CurlPin('example.test', 443, '93.184.216.34', static function (mixed $h, int $option, mixed $value) use (&$set): bool {
        $set[] = [$h, $option, $value];
        return true;
    }))($handle);
    expect($set)->toBe([[$handle, CURLOPT_RESOLVE, ['example.test:443:93.184.216.34']]]);
    // The real setter accepts the entry on a real handle.
    (new CurlPin('example.test', 443, '93.184.216.34'))(curl_init());
});

it('fails the request, as a Requests exception core turns into a WP_Error, when there is no handle or the option will not set', function (): void {
    expect(static fn() => (new CurlPin('example.test', 443, '93.184.216.34'))('not a handle'))->toThrow(\WpOrg\Requests\Exception::class);
    expect(static fn() => (new CurlPin('example.test', 443, '93.184.216.34', static fn(): bool => false))(curl_init()))->toThrow(\WpOrg\Requests\Exception::class);
});
```

```php
// ---------------------------------------------------------------- one lookup, one address, every hop
// The HTTP API is told to follow no redirect (redirection 0; WP 7.1 class-wp-http.php:359-363
// turns that into Requests' follow_redirects false, so a 3xx comes back as a response) and the
// tool follows them itself: each hop validated, looked up once, checked, and pinned into cURL by
// a CurlPin hooked on http_api_curl for that one request. Brain Monkey never runs a hooked
// callback, so these pin what is hooked and unhooked and with what entry; CurlPinTest pins what
// the callback does to a handle.

it('pins each hop to the address its check passed, hooking one CurlPin per request and unhooking it after', function (): void {
    Functions\when('wp_http_validate_url')->returnArg();
    $pins = [];
    Actions\expectAdded('http_api_curl')->twice()->whenHappen(static function (CurlPin $pin, int $priority, int $accepted) use (&$pins): void {
        $pins[] = [$pin->entry, $priority, $accepted];
    });
    Actions\expectRemoved('http_api_curl')->twice();
    $tool = webFetchTool(resolver: static fn(string $host): array => ['first.test' => ['93.184.216.34', '2606:2800:220:1::1'], 'next.test' => ['104.20.23.154']][$host]);
    Functions\expect('wp_safe_remote_get')->twice()->andReturnUsing(static function (string $url, array $args): array {
        expect($args['redirection'])->toBe(0);
        return match ($url) {
            'https://first.test/a' => webFetchResponse('', 301, '', ['location' => 'http://next.test:8080/b']),
            'http://next.test:8080/b' => webFetchResponse('<p>arrived</p>'),
        };
    });
    expect($tool->execute(['url' => 'https://first.test/a'])->content)->toBe('arrived')
        ->and($pins)->toBe([['first.test:443:93.184.216.34', 10, 1], ['next.test:8080:104.20.23.154', 10, 1]]);
});

it('asks the resolver once per hop and pins its first answer, so a name that rebinds between lookups never reaches the second', function (): void {
    Functions\when('wp_http_validate_url')->returnArg();
    $answers = [['93.184.216.34'], ['127.0.0.1']];
    $asked = 0;
    $tool = webFetchTool(resolver: static function (string $host) use (&$answers, &$asked): array {
        $asked++;
        return array_shift($answers) ?? [];

    expect($tool->execute(['url' => 'http://rebind.test/'])->content)->toBe('public')
        ->and($entry)->toBe('rebind.test:80:93.184.216.34')
        ->and($asked)->toBe(1);
});

it('checks every redirect target before it is requested, and refuses one core or the table refuses', function (): void {
    Functions\when('wp_http_validate_url')->alias(static fn(string $u): string|false => str_starts_with($u, 'ftp:') ? false : $u);
    $tool = webFetchTool(resolver: static fn(string $host): array => $host === 'internal.test' ? ['10.0.0.7'] : ['93.184.216.34']);
    foreach (['http://internal.test/', 'http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/', 'http://[::1]/', 'ftp://public.test/'] as $location) {

it('follows at most three redirects and refuses the fourth', function (): void {

    Functions\expect('wp_safe_remote_get')->times(4)->andReturnUsing(static fn(string $url): array => webFetchResponse('', 302, '', ['location' => $url . 'x/']));
    $res = webFetchTool()->execute(['url' => 'https://loop.test/']);
    expect($res->status)->toBe(ToolResultStatus::Error)
        ->and($res->content)->toContain('more than 3');
});

// A 3xx used to be followed by Requests, so what came back was never one. Now the tool sees
// every 3xx: one it follows needs a Location, and any other is an error, not a page to read.
it('reports a redirect with no Location, and a 3xx it does not follow, as an HTTP error rather than as a page', function (): void {
    Functions\when('wp_http_validate_url')->returnArg();
    foreach ([[302, []], [300, ['location' => 'https://x.test/']], [304, []]] as [$code, $headers]) {
        Functions\expect('wp_safe_remote_get')->once()->andReturn(webFetchResponse('<p>body</p>', $code, 'text/html', $headers));

            ->and($res->content)->toContain((string) $code)->not->toContain('body');
    }
});

it('pins neither an address literal nor the site\'s own host, and still fetches both', function (): void {

    Functions\expect('wp_safe_remote_get')->twice()->andReturn(webFetchResponse('<p>ok</p>'));
    $tool = webFetchTool(resolver: static fn(string $host): array => throw new LogicException("{$host} should not be looked up"));
    expect($tool->execute(['url' => 'http://93.184.216.34/'])->content)->toBe('ok')
        ->and($tool->execute(['url' => 'https://site.test/about/'])->content)->toBe('ok');
```

```php
    /**
     * A relative Location is resolved against the hop it came from by core's
     * WP_Http::make_absolute_url() (class-wp-http.php:973), which the unit suite does not load,
     * and followed by the tool itself, with the HTTP API's own redirects off on both requests.
     * Both hops are the site's own host, the one host the tool neither looks up nor pins

     */
    public function test_web_fetch_follows_a_relative_redirect_itself_with_the_http_apis_redirects_off(): void
    {
        $seen = [];
        add_filter('pre_http_request', static function (mixed $pre, array $args, string $url) use (&$seen): array {
            $seen[] = ['url' => $url, 'redirection' => $args['redirection']];
            if (count($seen) === 1) {
                return ['headers' => ['location' => '/moved/here/'], 'body' => '', 'response' => ['code' => 301, 'message' => 'Moved Permanently'], 'cookies' => [], 'filename' => null];
            }
            return ['headers' => ['content-type' => 'text/plain'], 'body' => 'arrived', 'response' => ['code' => 200, 'message' => 'OK'], 'cookies' => [], 'filename' => null];

        $res = $tool->execute(['url' => home_url('/old/')]);
        $this->assertSame(ToolResultStatus::Success, $res->status, $res->content);
        $this->assertSame('arrived', $res->content);
        $this->assertSame([
            ['url' => home_url('/old/'), 'redirection' => 0],
            ['url' => home_url('/moved/here/'), 'redirection' => 0],
        ], $seen);
```

`src/Toolkit/AddressRefused.php`:
```php
<?php

declare(strict_types=1);

namespace AlpacaBot\Toolkit;

/**
 * An address a model-chosen or admin-entered request may not go to, with a message fit to show
 * the person who entered it. Thrown by AddressPin; web_fetch answers with its own sentence
 * instead, the MCP settings screen shows this one.
 *
 * @since 0.6.0
 */
final class AddressRefused extends \RuntimeException {}
```

`src/Toolkit/AddressPin.php`:
```php
<?php

declare(strict_types=1);

namespace AlpacaBot\Toolkit;

/**
 * The one address rule the plugin's outbound requests share: resolve a name once, both
 * families, hold every answer to SpecialPurposeAddress, and hand back the single address the
 * connection is then pinned to. web_fetch pins it into cURL (CurlPin, through core's
 * `http_api_curl`); the MCP egress client pins it into Symfony HttpClient's `resolve` option
 * (Mcp\Egress). One rule, two transports.
 *
 * Why the address is returned and not just judged: a check that only answers yes or no leaves
 * the transport to resolve the name again when it connects, and a name under someone else's
 * control with a short TTL can answer the check with a public address and the connection with
 * 127.0.0.1 or the cloud metadata address (DNS rebinding; audit H-1). The caller connects to
 * the address this returns, so the answer that was checked is the answer that is used.
 *
 * Every answer has to pass, not just the one returned. The connection only goes to the first
 * one, but a name that answers with a private address among public ones is a name pointed at
 * something private, and refusing it costs a legitimate host nothing it had. A special-purpose
 * answer is let through only when core's `http_request_host_is_external` filter opts the host
 * in, so that opt-in means the same thing here as for any plugin. The first answer is the
 * first A record when there is one (lookup() lists A before AAAA): a dual-stack host is
 * reached over IPv4, which every server that can reach it at all can route.
 *
 * What this does not cover is the same as SpecialPurposeAddress: a public address the site's
 * own network routes somewhere private. And what it covers only holds while the caller really
 * connects to the returned address; a proxy that is sent the name resolves it again
 * (WebFetchToolkit and Mcp\Egress each say what they do about that).
 *
 * @since 0.6.0
 */
final class AddressPin
{
    /**
     * The address to connect to for `$host`, checked.
     *
     * @param string $host a host name or an IP literal (brackets allowed), as wp_parse_url() gives it
     * @param string $url the URL the host came from, for core's `http_request_host_is_external` filter; '' when there is none
     * @param null|\Closure(string): list<string> $lookup every address the name answers with; lookup() over the system resolver by default, a test hands in its own
     * @throws AddressRefused with a translated message naming the host
     */
    public static function resolve(string $host, string $url = '', ?\Closure $lookup = null): string
    {
        $host = strtolower(trim($host, '.'));
        $literal = trim($host, '[]');
        if ($literal === '') {
            throw new AddressRefused(__('The address has no host name.', 'alpaca-bot'));
        }
        $addresses = SpecialPurposeAddress::isAddress($literal) ? [$literal] : ($lookup ?? self::lookup(...))($host);
        if ($addresses === []) {
            /* translators: %s: a host name */
            throw new AddressRefused(sprintf(__('%s does not resolve, or its lookup failed.', 'alpaca-bot'), $host));
        }
        foreach ($addresses as $address) {
            if (!SpecialPurposeAddress::isAddress($address)) {
                /* translators: %s: a host name */
                throw new AddressRefused(sprintf(__('%s answered with something that is not an address.', 'alpaca-bot'), $host));
            }
            // phpcs:ignore WordPress.NamingConventions.PrefixAllGlobals.NonPrefixedHooknameFound -- Deliberately core's own filter, not a hook of ours: a site that has already told WordPress a private host is reachable should not have to say it again here.
            if (SpecialPurposeAddress::match($address) !== null && !apply_filters('http_request_host_is_external', false, $host, $url)) {
                /* translators: 1: a host name, 2: an IP address */
                throw new AddressRefused(sprintf(__('%1$s resolves to %2$s, a private, local or other special-purpose address.', 'alpaca-bot'), $host, $address));
            }
        }
        return $addresses[0];
    }

    /**
     * Every address a name answers with, both families, through the system resolver: A through
     * gethostbynamel(), AAAA through dns_get_record(). The connection is pinned to one of them,
     * but all of them are checked (resolve() says why), so both families have to be read. Cost:
     * two lookups, normally from the resolver's cache, since core's wp_http_validate_url() has
     * just made the A one; a resolver that times out costs its timeout. dns_get_record() warns
     * as well as returning false on a failed query, and a warning here is a refused request, not
     * an error worth logging, hence the suppression.
     *
     * A lookup that failed is not a lookup that answered "none". Either function returns false
     * when the query could not be made (a timeout, a nameserver that drops the question), and
     * that is answered with an empty list, which resolve() refuses as it refuses a name that
     * does not resolve. Read as "no records" instead, a false from the AAAA half would let a name
     * whose nameserver answers A with a public address and drops AAAA queries through on the A
     * alone. What the refusal costs: a legitimate host behind a resolver that fails one of the
     * two queries cannot be reached until the resolver answers.
     *
     * @param null|\Closure(string): (list<string>|false) $a    the A lookup; gethostbynamel() by default
     * @param null|\Closure(string): (array<mixed>|false) $aaaa the AAAA lookup; dns_get_record($host, DNS_AAAA) by default
     * @return list<string> every address, A first, or [] when the name has none or either lookup failed
     */
    public static function lookup(string $host, ?\Closure $a = null, ?\Closure $aaaa = null): array
    {
        $a ??= static fn(string $host): array|false => gethostbynamel($host);
        // phpcs:ignore WordPress.PHP.NoSilencedErrors.Discouraged -- dns_get_record() emits a PHP warning as well as returning false when a lookup fails. The false is checked below and refuses the request; the warning would only leak resolver detail into the response of an SSRF guard.
        $aaaa ??= static fn(string $host): array|false => @dns_get_record($host, DNS_AAAA);
        $v4 = $a($host);
        $v6 = $aaaa($host);
        if ($v4 === false || $v6 === false) {
            return [];
        }
        $addresses = array_values(array_filter($v4, 'is_string'));
        foreach ($v6 as $record) {
            if (is_array($record) && is_string($record['ipv6'] ?? null)) {
                $addresses[] = $record['ipv6'];
            }
        }
        return $addresses;
    }
}
```

`src/Toolkit/CurlPin.php`:
```php
<?php

declare(strict_types=1);

namespace AlpacaBot\Toolkit;

/**
 * One request's DNS pin for cURL: the `http_api_curl` callback web_fetch hooks for exactly one
 * wp_safe_remote_get() call and unhooks in a `finally`.
 *
 * Core fires `http_api_curl` from the Requests cURL transport's `curl.before_send`, with the
 * request's handle, after the handle is set up and before it runs (WP 7.1
 * class-wp-http-requests-hooks.php:56-58; Requests/src/Transport/Curl.php:172-174), and every
 * request gets a fresh handle (Requests.php:268-275, Curl.php:102-105), so the entry set here
 * dies with its request. CURLOPT_RESOLVE seeds that handle's DNS cache with `host:port:address`:
 * cURL connects to the address and still speaks to the host, so the Host header, TLS SNI and
 * certificate verification all use the name in the URL.
 *
 * The entry is applied to whatever handle the hook hands over, with no check that it belongs
 * to the request it was made for, and that is safe rather than careless: an entry for
 * `host:port` changes nothing for a request to any other host or port, and pins a request to
 * the same one to the address that was checked. Matching on the URL instead would be the
 * fragile choice, since core rewrites the URL (wp_kses_bad_protocol(), class-wp-http.php:287-289)
 * before it builds the hook's arguments.
 *
 * A handle the option cannot be set on fails the request: the exception is a Requests one,
 * which WP_Http::request() catches and returns as a WP_Error (class-wp-http.php:416-425), so an
 * unpinned connection is never the fallback (`class-wp-http.php:416`, `:424-425`). An IPv6
 * address goes in unbracketed, as Symfony
 * HttpClient writes the same option (CurlHttpClient.php:199).
 *
 * @since 0.6.0
 */
final class CurlPin
{
    /** The CURLOPT_RESOLVE entry: `host:port:address`. */
    public readonly string $entry;

    /**
     * @param null|\Closure(mixed, int, mixed): bool $setopt curl_setopt() by default; a test hands in a recorder
     */
    public function __construct(string $host, int $port, string $ip, private ?\Closure $setopt = null)
    {
        $this->entry = $host . ':' . $port . ':' . $ip;
    }

    /** The `http_api_curl` callback (accepted args: 1, the handle). */
    public function __invoke(mixed $handle): void
    {
        // phpcs:ignore WordPress.WP.AlternativeFunctions.curl_curl_setopt -- This is a callback on core's own http_api_curl action, whose whole purpose is setting options on the HTTP API's cURL handle; there is no WordPress wrapper for CURLOPT_RESOLVE.
        $setopt = $this->setopt ?? static fn(mixed $h, int $option, mixed $value): bool => curl_setopt($h, $option, $value);
        if (!$handle instanceof \CurlHandle || !$setopt($handle, CURLOPT_RESOLVE, [$this->entry])) {
            throw new \WpOrg\Requests\Exception(__('The request could not be held to the address that was checked.', 'alpaca-bot'), 'alpaca_bot.pin_failed');
        }
    }
}
```

`src/Toolkit/WebFetchToolkit.php`:

(a) Replace the class docblock paragraphs at `:17-48`, from "The model chooses the URL, so the fetch is held to two checks in turn…" through "…whatever name it resolved.", with:
```php
 * The model chooses the URL, so every hop of the fetch, the URL itself and then each redirect up
 * to MAX_REDIRECTS of them, is held to two checks in turn, and a hop has to pass both. Core's
 * first: wp_http_validate_url(), which refuses anything but http(s), a URL with credentials, a
 * port outside 80/443/8080, and a host that is or resolves to what the running core version
 * knows to be local. What core knows depends on its version: 6.9, the plugin's floor, refuses
 * loopback, RFC 1918 and 0/8 and passes the link-local address where a cloud instance's
 * metadata service answers with the instance's credentials; 7.1 refuses the IPv4 registry; no
 * version reads an AAAA record. So the plugin's own check runs second, in AddressPin: one
 * lookup, both families, every answer held to SpecialPurposeAddress, one address handed back.
 * Two of core's rules are kept on purpose, and pin() says what each costs: the site's own host
 * is exempt, and `http_request_host_is_external` opts a host in for this tool as it does for
 * any plugin; `http_allowed_safe_ports` is core's and stays core's. Core's check is asked here
 * as well as inside the request so that the refusal is the tool's own message, not a WP_Error
 * about a blocked URL, and so that nothing is built for a URL that was never going anywhere.
 *
 * The address the check passed is the address the connection uses. Each check is its own DNS
 * lookup and the transport would make one more when it connects, so a name under an attacker's
 * control with a short TTL could answer the checks with a public address and the connection
 * with 127.0.0.1 or the metadata address (DNS rebinding; audit H-1), and the response text
 * would come back to the model. So the transport is not asked: a CurlPin hands the checked
 * address to cURL as CURLOPT_RESOLVE through core's `http_api_curl` action, hooked for the one
 * request and unhooked in a `finally` (get()). Redirects are the same problem once per hop, so
 * the HTTP API is told to follow none (`redirection` 0, which core turns into Requests'
 * `follow_redirects` false, WP 7.1 class-wp-http.php:359-363, so a 3xx comes back as a
 * response) and this class follows them itself, each hop validated, looked up, checked and
 * pinned like the first. Core's wp_http_validate_url() inside the request still makes a lookup
 * of its own; it can only refuse, and the connection never uses its answer.
 *
 * What the pin does not reach. A proxy configured for the HTTP API (WP_PROXY_HOST) is sent the
 * name and resolves it itself (class-wp-http.php:401-410), so the pin stops at the proxy, and
 * what the proxy may reach, this tool may reach. The site's own host is neither looked up nor
 * pinned (pin()). A public address that the site's own network routes somewhere private is no
 * address check's to see (SpecialPurposeAddress).
```
